DSA

Analysis of Algorithms

Understanding the time and space complexity of algorithms is the cornerstone of writing efficient, professional software. This section covers core concepts of asymptotic notation, loop analysis, and recurrences.

Definition

Algorithm Analysis is the process of determining the amount of time (time complexity) and memory space (space complexity) resources required to execute a given algorithm relative to the input size.

Why Analyze Algorithms?

  • To predict performance behavior without implementing it on a specific computer (which varies due to hardware variations).
  • To establish a clean, machine-independent measure of efficiency.
  • To compare different algorithms solving the same problem and choose the optimal approach.

Asymptotic Notation

Asymptotic analysis allows us to measure the order of growth of an algorithm as the input size $n$ grows towards infinity. There are three key mathematical bounds:

Big-O (O)
  • Represents the Upper Bound.
  • Guarantees that an algorithm will not take longer than this limit.
  • Def: $f(n) \le c \cdot g(n)$ for all $n \ge n_0$.
Omega (Ω)
  • Represents the Lower Bound.
  • Guarantees the minimum resources the algorithm will consume.
  • Def: $f(n) \ge c \cdot g(n)$ for all $n \ge n_0$.
Theta (Θ)
  • Represents the Tight Bound.
  • Occurs when upper and lower bounds grow at the same rate.
  • Def: $c_1 \cdot g(n) \le f(n) \le c_2 \cdot g(n)$.

Analysis of Common Loops

1. Linear Increments / Decrements

Python
O(b) O(1) 
def add(a, b):
    result = a
    while b > 0:
        result += 1
        b -= 1
    return result

2. Fractional Increments

Python
O(a/b) O(1) 
def subtract(a, b):
    result = a
    count = 0
    while result >= b:
        result -= b
        count += 1
    return count

3. Logarithmic Scaling

Python
O(log n) O(1) 
def multiply(n, c):
    result = 1
    while result < n:
        result *= c
    return result

4. Log-Log Scaling

Python
O(log log n) O(1) 
def exponentfun(n, c):
    result = 2
    while result < n:
        result = result ** c
    return result

Recursive Complexity & Recurrence Relations

Many algorithms are recursive. When analyzing them, we express their time complexity using Recurrence Relations. Example: $T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n$. We resolve these using three primary methods:

1. Substitution Method

We guess the solution (e.g., $T(n) = O(n \log n)$) and then use mathematical induction to prove that the guess is correct.

2. Recurrence Tree Method

We draw the recursion tree, find the work done at each level, and sum the work across all levels. This gives us a visual representation of how execution splits and gathers.

3. Master Method

A direct template formula for recurrences of the form $T(n) = aT(n/b) + f(n)$ where $a \ge 1$ and $b > 1$.

Space Complexity vs. Auxiliary Space

  • Space Complexity: Total space taken by the algorithm, including the input arguments.
  • Auxiliary Space: Extra space or temporary space used by the algorithm. In interviews, we focus heavily on Auxiliary Space.
Python Recursive Space Example
O(n) O(n) (Call Stack) 
def add_recursion(n):
    if n <= 0:
        return 0
    return n + add_recursion(n - 1)

Mathematics

Case Example

Input: 5
Output: 15
Explanation: 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 15

Python Implementation
O(n) O(1)
# simple solution

def findSum(n) :
	sum = 0
	x = 1
	while x <=n :
		sum = sum + x
		x = x + 1
	return sum

# Driver code
n = 5
print(findSum(n))
Python Implementation
O(1) O(1)
# Efficient Solution
def findSum(n) :
	return n * (n + 1) / 2

# Driver code
n = 50
print(findSum(n))
Python Implementation 
# To find the sum of first n natural numbers that avoid overflow if the result is going to be within limits.

# Returns sum of first n natural numbers
def findSum(n) :
	if (n % 2 == 0) :
		return (n / 2) * (n + 1)

# If n is odd, (n+1) must be even
	else :
	  return ((n + 1) / 2) * n

# Driver code
n = 51
print(findSum(n))

Palindrome Number

Case Example

I/P = 78987
O/P = 78987

Python Implementation
Theta(n)
def ispal(n):
  rev = 0
  x = n
  while x != 0:
    rev = rev * 10 + x % 10
    x = x // 10
  return rev == n, x

print(ispal(12321))
print(ispal(42322))

# Aux Space : O(1)

Factorial Number

Case Example

I/P = 3
O/P = 6

3! = 3 * 2 * 1

Python Implementation
O(n)
# Recursive Approach

def func(n):
  if n == 0:
    return 1
  return (n*func(n-1))

print(func(3))

# Aux Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(n)
# Iterative Approach

def fact(n):
  if n < 0:
    return 0

  elif n == 0 or n == 1:
    return 1

  else:
    fact = 1
    while n > 1:
      fact *= n
      n -= 1
    return fact

print(fact(3))

# Aux Space : O(n)

Trailing Zeros in Factorial

we given a number. The task is to find the Number of Trailing Zeros in the factorial of the Number

Trailing Zeros are the Zeros, which appear at the end of a number

Case Example

Input: 50
Output: 1 // 5! = 5 * 4 * 3 * 2 * 1

Python Implementation
O(log_5 n)
def find(n):
  if n < 0:
    return -1

  count = 0
  i = 5
  while n // i >= 1:
    count += n // i
    i *= 5

  return count

print(find(100))

# Aux Space : O(1)

GCD or HCF of two Numbers

GCD : Greatest Common Divisor

HCF : Highest Common Factor

It is largest number that divides both of them

Case Example

Input: a = 20; b = 28
Output: 4

Factors

20 : 1,2,4,5,10,20

28 : 1,2,3,4,7,14,28

Among all greatest factors we have 4 is common

Python Implementation
O(min(a,b))
# Naive Approach

def  find(a,b):
  result = min(a,b)

  while result > 0:
    if a % result == 0 and b % result == 0:
      break
    result -= 1
  return result

print(find(20,28))

# Aux Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(log(min(a,b)))
# Euclidean Approach : Recursive Approach

def find(a,b):
  if b == 0:
    return a
  return find(b,a % b)

print(find(20,28))

# Aux Space : O(log(min(a,b)))
Python Implementation
O(log(min(a,b)))
# Optimised Euclidean Approach : Iterative Approach

def find(a,b):
  while a != b:
    if a > b:
      a = a - b
    else:
      b = b - a
  return a

print(find(20,28))

# Aux Space : O(1)

LCM of two number

LCM : Least Common Multiple

It is the smallest numner that can be divided by both Numbers.

Case Example

Input: a = 15; b = 20
Output: 60

Python Implementation
O(a*b - max(a,b))
# Naive Approach

def LCM(a,b):
  res = max(a,b)

  while True:
    if res % a == 0 and res % b == 0:
      return res
    res += 1

print(LCM(15,20))

# Aux Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(log(min(a,b)))
# Efficient Solution

def GCD(a,b):
  if b == 0:
    return a
  return GCD(b,a % b)

def LCM(a,b):
  return abs(a * b) / GCD(a,b)

print(LCM(15,20))

# Aux Space : O(log(min(a,b))

Check for Prime

Case Example

Input: 11
Output: True

Python Implementation
O(n)
# Naive Approach

def prime(a):
  if a == 1:
    return False
  for i in range(2,a):
    if a % i == 0:
      return False
  return True

print(prime(11))

# Aux Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(sqrt(n))
# Efficient Approach

def prime(a):
  if a <= 1:
    return False
  if a <= 3:
    return True
  if a % 2 == 0 or a % 3 == 0:
    return False
  i = 5
  while i * i <= a:
    if a % i == 0 or a % (i + 2) == 0:
      return False
    i = i + 6
  return True

print(prime(10))

# Aux Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(sqrt(n))
# Super Efficient

def prime(n):
  if n == 1:
    return False

  if n == 2 or n == 3:
    return True

  if n % 2 == 0 or n % 3 == 0:
    return False

  i = 5
  while i * i <= n:
    if n % i == 0 or n % (i + 2) == 0:
      return False
    i = i + 6
  return True

print(prime(11))

# Aux Space : O(1)

Prime Factorization

Prime factorization is finding which prime numbers multiply together to create the original number. It involves breaking down a number into its prime number components.

100 : 2^2 * 5^2

Case Example

Input: 100
Output: 2 2 5 5

Python Implementation
O(sqrt(n))
def is_prime(num):
    if num <= 1:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def prime_factors(n):
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        if is_prime(i):
            while n % i == 0:
                print(i, end=' ')
                n //= i

number = int(input("Enter a number: "))
print("Prime factors of", number, "are:", end=' ')
prime_factors(number)

# Aux Space : O(1)

All Divisors of a Number

Given a natural number n, print all distinct divisors of it.

Case Example

Input: n = 10
Output: 1 2 5 10

Python Implementation
O(n)
# Naive Solution

def Div(n):
  for i in range(1,n+1):
    if n % i == 0:
      print(i, end = ' ')

Div(10)

# Aux Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(sqrt(n))
# Better than Naive Solution : Unsorted Way

import math

def Div(n):
  i = 1
  while i <= math.sqrt(n):
    if n % i == 0:
      if n // i == i:
        print(i,end=' ')
      else:
        print(i,n//i,end=' ')
    i += 1

Div(100)

# Aux Space : O(1)
Python Implementation 
# Better than Naive Solution : Sorted Way

def print1(n):
  i = 1
  while i*i < n:
    if n % i == 0:
      print(i, end=' ')
    i += 1
  while i >= 1:
    if n % i == 0:
      print(n//i, end=' ')
    i -= 1

print1(100)

Sieve of Eratosthenes

It is about to find all the prime numbers less than or equal to a given number.

Case Example

Input: n = 10
Output: 2 3 5 7

Naive Solution: Try dividing with all the number s

Efficient Solution:

  • Divisors always appear in pairs.
  • One of the divisors in every pair is smaller than or equal to sqrt(n).
  • Print all divisors but not necessarily in order.
Python Implementation
O(n * sqrt(n))
# Naive Solution

def pr(n):
  for i in range(2, n+1):
    if prime(i):
      print(i, end = ' ')

print(pr(7))

# Aux Space : Theta(1)
Python Implementation 
# Efficient Approach

def seive(n):
  if n <= 1:
    return
  isprime = [True] * (n+1)
  i = 2
  while i * i <= n:
    if isprime[i] == True:
      for j in range(i * i, n+1, i):
        isprime[j] = False

    i += 1
  for i in range(2,n+1):
    if isprime[i]:
      print(i, end = ' ')

seive(50)

Computing Power

Case Example

Input: x = 2, n = 3
Output: 8

Python Implementation
O(n)
# Naive Approach

def power(x,n):
  pow = 1

  for _ in range(n):
    pow *= x
  return pow

print(power(2,3))

# Aux Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(log(n))
# Efficient Approach

def power(x,y):
  temp = 0
  if y == 0:
    return 1
  temp = power(x,int(y/2))
  if y % 2 == 0:
    return temp * temp
  else:
    if y > 0:
      return x * temp * temp
    else:
      return (temp * temp) / x

print(power(2,3))

# Aux Space : O(log(n))

Iterative Power (Binary Exponentiation)

  • Every number can be written as sum of powers of 2.
  • We can travelers through all bits of a number in O(log n)
Case Example

Input: x = 3, y = 5
Output: 243

Python Implementation
O(log(n))
# Iterative Power

def power(x,n):
  res = 1
  while n > 0:
    if n % 2 == 0:
      x = x * x
      n = n // 2
    else:
      res = res * x
      n = n - 1
  return res

print(power(3,5))

# If list contain any other string than int, then the code will not work

Working of list in Python

Basically list underlying the array data structure where contiguous memory location happens.

It means there is a pre-allocated extra space for future additions.

index = 0, 1, 2, 3, .....

values = 2, 'p', '1', 'True' .....

Advantages:

  • Random Access - easily get item in constant time
  • cache friendly - locality of reference
  • when we search for one data point it also store the reference of others if we have contiguous allocation.

Disadvantages:

  • Insertion, Deletion, Search are slower because if any item added, deleted then we need to change the index position and move

How doe Dynamic size work...?

  • pre allocate some extra space
  • If it becomes full, do the following

1. Allocate a new space of larger size (multply by size of list * 2)

2. copy old space to new

3. Freeup the old space

Case Example

Explanation:

list = []

for i in range(1, 1001):

  • l.append(i)

Assumption :

1. initially space for 10 items or allocated

2. when a number is inserted at 11th index then block of '10' will be deallocated and then allocates double sized

Time Complexity : O(1) # constant which is Amortized Time

Average or Mean of a list

Case Example

Input: l = [10, 20, 30, 40]
Output: 25.0

Python Implementation 
l = [10,20,30,40]
def avg(l):
  sum = 0
  for i in l:
    sum += i
  n = len(l)
  return sum/n

print(avg(l))

Seperate Even and Odd

Case Example

Input: l = [10, 20, 11, 40, 15, 19]
Output:

even = [10, 20, 40]

odd = [11, 15, 19]

Python Implementation 
def getevenodd(l):
  even = []
  odd = []
  for i in l:
    if i%2 == 0:
      even.append(i)
    else:
      odd.append(i)
  return even, odd

l = [10, 20, 11, 40, 15, 19]
even, odd = getevenodd(l)
print(even)
print(odd)

Get Smaller Elements

Case Example

Input: l = [10, 20, 11, 40, 15, 19], x = 21
Output: [10, 20, 11, 15, 19]

Python Implementation 
def getSmaller(x):
  res = []
  for e in l:
    if e < x:
      res.append(e)
  return res

l = [10, 20, 11, 40, 15, 19]
x = 21
print(getSmaller(x))

Comprehensions

List comprehension provides a shortcut syntax to create a list from another iterable.

Iterable : A range function numbers are iterables

syntax : list = [ item for item in collection if condition ]

Python Implementation 
l = [ x for x in range(11)]
print(l)

l = [ i for i in range(11) if i%2 == 0]
print(l)

l = [ x for x in range(11) if x%2 == 1]
print(l)
Python Implementation 
# Get Smaller

def getSmaller(x):
  return [e for e in l if e < x]

l = [10, 20, 11, 40, 15, 19]
x = 21
print(getSmaller(x))
Python Implementation 
# Get Even Odd

def getEvenOdd(l):
  even = [i for i in l if i%2 == 0]
  odd = [i for i in l if i%2 == 1]
  return even, odd

l = [10, 20, 11, 40, 15, 19]
even, odd = getEvenOdd(l)
print(even)
print(odd)
Python Implementation

print(l)

Largest Element in a List

Case Example

Input: l = [10, 5, 20, 8]
Output: 20

Python Implementation
O(n^2)
# Naive

l = [10, 2, 5, 8, 20, 19]

def getmax(l):
  for x in l:
    for y in l:
      if y < x:
        break
    else:
      return x
  return None

print(getmax(l))
Python Implementation
O(n)
# Efficient

def getmax(l):
  if not l:
    return None
  else:
    res = l[0]
    for i in range(1, len(l)):
      if l[i] > res:
        res = l[i]
    return res

print(getmax(l))

Second Largest Element in a List

Case Example

Input: l = [10, 20, 4, 5, 6, 1]
Output: 10

Python Implementation
Theta(n)
def secondLargest(l):
  if len(l) <= 1:
    return None

  lar = getMax(l)
  slar = None

  for x in l:
    if x != lar:
      if slar == None:
        slar = x
      else:
        slar = max(slar,x)
  return slar

def getMax(l):
  res = l[0]
  for i in range(1, len(l)):
    res = max(res, l[i])
  return res


l = [10, 20, 4, 5, 6, 1]
print(secondLargest(l))
Python Implementation
Theta(n)
# Efficient

def secondLargest(l):
  if len(l) <= 1:
    return None
  lar = l[0]
  slar = None
  for x in l[1:]:
    if x > lar:
      slar = lar
      lar = x
    elif x != lar:
      if slar == None or slar < x:
        slar = x
  return slar

l = [10, 20, 4, 5, 6, 1]
print(secondLargest(l))

Check if a List is sorted

Case Example

Input: l = [10, 20, 30]
Output: Yes

Python Implementation 
# Naive

def isSorted(l):
  copy = sorted(l)
  if copy == l:
    return True
  else:
    return False

l = [10, 20, 30]
print(isSorted(l))

l = [29, 24, 32]
print(isSorted(l))
Python Implementation
O(n)
# Efficient

def ifSort(l):
  i = 1
  while i < len(l):
    if l[i] < l[i-1]:
      return False
    i += 1
  return True

l = [10, 20, 30]
print(ifSort(l))

l = [29, 24, 32]
print(ifSort(l))

Reverse of a List

Case Example

Python Implementation 
# Method 2

l = [10, 20, 30]
print(l[::-1])
Python Implementation 
# Method 3

l = [10, 20, 30]
res = []
for i in range(len(l)-1, -1, -1):
  res.append(l[i])
print(res)
Python Implementation 
# Method 4

l = [10, 20, 30]
res = list(reversed(l))
print(res)
Python Implementation
O(n)
# Reduce half Time

def reverse(l):
  s = 0
  e = len(l) - 1
  while s < e:
    l[s], l[e] = l[e], l[s]
    s += 1
    e -= 1

l = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]
reverse(l)
print(l)

Remove Duplicates from a sorted Array list

Case Example

Input: arr[] = {10, 20, 20, 30, 30, 30, 30}

size = 7

Case Example

Output: arr[] = {10, 20, 30, _, _, _, _ }

size = 3

Python Implementation
O(n)
# Naive

def remdup(arr, n):
  temp = [0] * n
  temp[0] = arr[0]
  res = 1
  for i in range(1, n):
    if temp[res-1] != arr[i]:
      temp[res] = arr[i]
      res += 1
  return res, temp

arr = [10, 20, 20, 30, 30, 30, 30]
n = len(arr)
no, temp = remdup(arr, n)
print(temp)
print("size : ", no)

# Auxiliary Space : O(n)
Python Implementation
O(n)
# Efficient

def remdup(arr, n):
  arr.sort()
  res = 1
  for i in range(1, n):
    if arr[i] != arr[res-1]:
      arr[res] = arr[i]
      res += 1
  return res

l = [10, 20, 20, 30, 40, 30, 30]
n = len(l)
print(remdup(l, n))

Left Rotate (Counter Clockwise) by One

Python Implementation 
# Method 2

l = [10, 20, 30, 40]
l.append(l.pop(0))
print(l)
Python Implementation 
# Own Logic

def rotateByOne(l):
  n = len(l)
  x = l[0]
  for i in range(1, n):
    l[i-1] = l[i]
  l[n-1] = x

l = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]
rotateByOne(l)
print(l)
Python Implementation 
# Chicken Leg

def farn(T, cases):

    for i in range(T):
        X, Y, Z = cases[i]
        chickens = (Z % X == 0)
        ducks = (Z % Y == 0)

        if chickens and ducks:
            print("Any")
        elif chickens:
            print("Chicken")
        elif ducks:
            print("Duck")
        else:
            print("Null")

T = int(input("Cases : "))
stack = []

for i in range(T):
    X, Y, Z = map(int, input().strip().split())
    stack.append([X, Y, Z])

print(stack)

results = farn(T, stack)

Recursion

A Function calls itself directly or indirectly

Direct Method:

def func();

  • func()

Indirect Method

def fun1():

  • fun2()

def fun2();

  • fun1()
Python Implementation 
def fun():
  for i in range(1):
    print("GFG")
    break

fun()

Recursion Background

Python Implementation 
def fun1():
  print("Function 1 is called")

fun1()
Python Implementation 
def fun2():
  print("Before Function 1")
  fun1()
  print("After Function 1")

print("Before Function 2")
fun2()
print("After Function 2")

Typical structure of Reecursive Functions

def fun(...):

  • base cases
  • Recursive cal : with atleast one change to parameter so that call approches towards base case

Applications of Recursion

1. Many algorithm techniques are based on recursive

  • Dynamic Programming
  • Backtracking
  • Divide and Conquer (Binary Search, Merge Sort, Quick Sort)

2. Many Problems inherently recursive

  • Tower of Hanoi
  • DFS based traversals (DFS of graph and Inorder, Post Order, Pre Order traverse)
Python Implementation 
# Factorial

def fact(n):
  if n == 0:    # base case
    return 1
  return n * fact(n-1) # recursive case

fact(5)

# Note : Don't use n == 1 because it goes to -ve infinity
Python Implementation 
# Fibbonacci : nth value of series

# 0 1 1 2 3 5 8 12 20

def fib(n):
  if n == 0 or n == 1:
    return n
  return fib(n-1) + fib(n-2)

fib(10)

Tail Recursion

A recursive function is called tail recursive if the function does not do anything after the last recursive call.

Python Implementation 
def fun(n):
  if n == 0:
    return
  print(n)
  fun(n-1)   # Tail Recursive function

fun(5)
Python Implementation 
# Not Tail Recursion

def fun(n):
  if n == 0:
    return
  fun(n-1)
  print(n)   # Not Tail Recursive function

fun(5)

Removing Recursion

1. Replace if with while

2. Change value of parameters at the end of loop

Examples of Tail Recursive Functions

  • Quick Sort
  • Post Order Tree Traversal
Python Implementation 
def fun(n):
  if n == 0:
    return
  print(n, end = ' ')
  fun(n-1)
  print(n, end = ' ')

fun(4)
Python Implementation 
def fun(n):
  if n == 0:
    return
  fun(n-1)
  print(n, end = ' ')
  fun(n-1)

fun(4)
Python Implementation 
# Calculates the number of times you can divide n by 2

def fun(n):
  if n <= 1:
    return 0
  else:
    return 1 + fun(n//2)

fun(16)

# What does this funtion do in general for n >= 1 ?

# log_2(n)
Python Implementation 
# Shows the Binary Representaion of a number

def fun(n):
  if n == 0:
    return
  fun(n//2)
  print(n%2, end = '')

fun(13)

Palindrome Check

Case Example

Input: 'aabbaa'

O/p : False

Python Implementation
O(n) O(n)
def palin(str, start, end):
  if start >= end:
    return True
  return str[start] == str[end] and palin(str, start+1, end-1)

palin('aabbaa', 0, 5)

Searching

Case Example

Input: [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]

x = 2

Case Example

O/P = 1

Python Implementation 
# Naive Solution

def binary(l,x):
  low = 0
  high = len(l) - 1
  while low <= high:
    mid = (low + high) // 2
    if l[mid] == x:
      return mid
    elif l[mid] < x:
      low = mid + 1
    else:
      high = mid - 1

  return -1

l = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60]
x = 40
print(binary(l,x))
Python Implementation 
# Recursive Binary Search

def binary(l, x, low, high):
  if low > high:
    return -1
  mid = (low + high) // 2
  if l[mid] == x:
    return mid
  elif l[mid] < x:
    return binary(l, x, mid + 1, high)
  else:
    return binary(l, x, low, mid - 1)

l = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60]
x = 50

print(binary(l, x, 0, len(l) - 1))

Bineay Search for Unsorted List

Python Implementation 
# Method 1

aux_space = []

def make_aux(arr, n):
  global aux_space
  for i in range(n):
    aux_space.append(arr[i])

  aux_space.sort()

l = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 9, 10, 22, 54, 21, 29]
make_aux(l, len(l))
print(binary(aux_space, 9, 0, len(aux_space) - 1))
print(aux_space)
Python Implementation 
# Method 2

def binary_search(arr, target):
    left, right = 0, len(arr) - 1

    while left <= right:
        mid = left + (right - left) // 2 # avoiding the Overflow
        if arr[mid] == target:
            return mid
        elif arr[mid] < target:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid - 1
    return -1

arr = [3, 6, 1, 9, 4, 2]
arr.sort()
res = binary_search(arr, 3)
print(res)
Python Implementation 
# Method 3

def findPeak(nums):
  left, right = 0 ,len(nums) - 1

  while left < right:
    mid = left + (right - left) // 2
    if nums[mid] < nums[mid + 1]:
      left = mid + 1
    else:
      right = mid
  return left

nums = [1, 2, 1, 3, 5, 6, 4]
print(findPeak(nums))

Index of First Occurrence : Sorted Order

Case Example

Input: arr[] = [1, 10, 20, 10, 20, 30, 30, 10 ]

x = 20

Case Example

Output: 2

For the first occurrence of a number

1. If (high >= low)

2. Calculate mid = low + (high - low)/2;

3. If ((mid == 0 || x > arr[mid-1]) && arr[mid] == x) : return mid;

4. Else if (x > arr[mid]) : return first(arr, (mid + 1), high, x, n);

5. Else : return first(arr, low, (mid -1), x, n);

6. Otherwise return -1;

Python Implementation
O(n)
# Naive : Unsorted & Sorted Order

def firstOcc(arr, n, x):
  for i in range(0, n):
    if arr[i] == x:
      return i
  return - 1

l = [1, 10, 20, 10, 20, 30, 30, 10]
n = len(l)
x = 20
print(firstOcc(l, n, x))

# Auxiliary Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(logn)
# Efficient : Using Recursion

def firstOcc(arr, low, high, x, n):
  if low > high:
    return -1

  if(high >= low):
    mid = low + (high - low) // 2
    if((mid == 0 or x > arr[mid - 1]) and arr[mid] == x):
      return mid
    elif(x > arr[mid]):
      return firstOcc(arr, (mid + 1), high, x, n)
    else:
      return firstOcc(arr, low, (mid - 1), x, n)

  return -1

l = [10, 10, 20, 30, 40, 40, 40, 40]

n = len(l)
x = 20
print(firstOcc(l, 0, n - 1, x, n))

# Auxiliary Space : O(n)
Python Implementation
O(log n) O(1)
# Efficient using Iteration

def first(arr, n, x):
  low = 0
  high = n - 1
  while low <= high:
    mid = (low + high) // 2
    if x > arr[mid]:
      low = mid + 1
    elif x < arr[mid]:
      high = mid - 1
    else:
      if mid == 0 or x > arr[mid - 1]:
        return mid
      else:
        high = mid - 1

  return -1

l = [10, 10, 20, 30, 40, 40, 40, 40]
n = len(l)
x = 20
print(first(l, n, x))

Index of Last Occurrence : Sorted Order

Case Example

Input: arr[] = [1, 10, 20, 10, 20, 30, 30, 10 ]

x = 20

Case Example

Output: 4

For the Last occurrence of a number

1. if (high >= low)

2. calculate mid = low + (high - low)/2;

3. if( ( mid == n-1 || x < arr[mid+1]) && arr[mid] == x ) : return mid;

4. else if(x < arr[mid]) : return last(arr, low, (mid -1), x, n);

5. else : return last(arr, (mid + 1), high, x, n);

6. otherwise return -1;

Python Implementation
O(n)
# Naive : Unsorted & Sorted Orders

def findLastOccurrence(arr, n, x):
    last = -1
    for i in range(n):
        if arr[i] == x:
            last = i

    if last != -1:
        print("Last Occurrence = ", last)
    else:
        print("Not Found")

l = [10, 20, 30, 20, 30, 40, 40, 40]
n = len(l)
x = 30
findLastOccurrence(l, n, x)

# Auxiliary Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(log n)
# Efficient : Using Recursion

def last(arr, low, high, x, n):
	if (high >= low):
		mid = low + (high - low) // 2
		if ((mid == n - 1 or x < arr[mid + 1]) and arr[mid] == x):
			return mid
		elif (x < arr[mid]):
			return last(arr, low, (mid - 1), x, n)
		else:
			return last(arr, (mid + 1), high, x, n)

	return -1


arr = [1, 2, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4, 7, 8, 8]
n = len(arr)

x = 8
print("Last Occurrence = ", last(arr, 0, n - 1, x, n))

# Auxiliary Space : O(n)

Count Occurences in a Sorted Array

Case Example

Input: l = [10, 20, 20, 20, 30, 30]

x = 20

Case Example

Output: 3

NOTE :

Naive Solution : Linear Traversal : O(n)

Better Solution : Simple Binary search : O(logn + K)

Python Implementation
O(log n) O(1)
# Better Solution

def search(nums, target, find_start_index):
    answer = -1
    start = 0
    end = len(nums) - 1
    while start <= end:
        mid = start + (end - start) // 2
        if target < nums[mid]:
            end = mid - 1
        elif target > nums[mid]:
            start = mid + 1
        else:
            answer = mid
            if find_start_index:
                end = mid - 1
            else:
                start = mid + 1
    return answer

def count_occurrences(nums, x):
    first_index = search(nums, x, True)
    last_index = search(nums, x, False)
    if first_index != -1 and last_index != -1:
        return last_index - first_index + 1
    else:
        return 0

if __name__ == "__main__":
    arr = [1, 2, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4, 7, 8, 8]
    x = 2
    c = count_occurrences(arr, x)
    print(c)
Python Implementation
O(logn)
# Efficient

def firstOcc(arr, x):
  n = len(arr)
  for i in range(0, n):
    if arr[i] == x:
      return i
  return - 1

def lastOcc(arr, x):
  n = len(arr)
  for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
    if arr[i] == x:
      return i
  return -1

def countOcc(l, x):
  first = firstOcc(l, x)
  if first == -1:
    return 0
  else:
    return lastOcc(l, x) - first + 1

l = [10, 20, 20, 20, 20, 20, 20, 30, 30]
x = 20
print(countOcc(l, x))

# Auxiliary Space : O(n)

Count 1's in a Sorted Binary List

Case Example

Input: arr[] = {1, 1, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0}
Output: 2
Input: arr[] = {1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1}
Output: 7
Input: arr[] = {0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0}
Output: 0

Naive approach:

A simple solution is to linearly traverse the array until we find the 1's in the array and keep count of 1s. If the array element becomes 0 then return the count of 1's.

Time Complexity: O(n)

Auxiliary Space: O(1)

Efficient : Count 1's in a sorted binary array using Binary search recursively

Do while low <= high:

  • Calculate mid using low + (high - low) / 2.
  • Check if the element at mid index is the last 1
  • If the element is not last 1, move the low to right side recursively and return the result received from it.
  • Otherwise, move the low to left recursively and return the result received from it.
Python Implementation
O(log(n))
def countOnes(arr, low, high):
    if high >= low:
        mid = low + (high-low)//2
        if ((mid == high or arr[mid+1] == 0) and (arr[mid] == 1)):
            return mid+1
        if arr[mid] == 1:
            return countOnes(arr, (mid+1), high)
        return countOnes(arr, low, mid-1)

    return 0

arr = [1, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0]
print("Count of 1's in given array is", countOnes(arr, 0, len(arr)-1))

# Auxiliary Space: O(log(n))

Efficient : Count 1's in a sorted binary array using binary search iteratively

Do while low <= high:

  • Calculate the middle index say mid
  • Check if arr[mid] is less than 1 then move the high to left side (i.e, hight = mid - 1)
  • If the element is not last 1 then move the low to the right side (i.e, low = mid + 1)
  • Check if the element at the middle index is last 1 then return mid + 1

Otherwise move to low to right (i.e, low = mid + 1)

Python Implementation
O(log(n))
def countOnes(arr, n):
    low = 0
    high = n - 1
    while (low <= high):
        mid = (low + high) // 2
        if ((mid == n - 1 or arr[mid + 1] == 0) and arr[mid] == 1):
            return mid + 1
        if (arr[mid] == 0):
            high = mid - 1
        elif(arr[mid] == 1):
            low = mid + 1

    return 0


if __name__ == '__main__':
    arr = [1, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0]
    n = len(arr)
    print("Count of 1's in given array is ", countOnes(arr, n))

# Auxiliary Space: O(1)

Square Root

Case Example

Input: x = 4
Output: 2

Python Implementation
O(sqrt(x))
# Naive

def sqrtt(x):
  i = 1
  while i*i <= x:
    i += 1
  return i - 1

print(sqrtt(4))

# Auxiliary Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(logn) O(1)
# Efficient

def sqrt(x):
  low = 1
  high = x
  ans = -1

  while low <= high:
    mid = (low + high) // 2
    msq = mid * mid
    if msq == x:
      return mid
    elif msq < x:
      low = mid + 1
      ans = mid
    else:
      high = mid - 1
  return ans

print(sqrt(4))

Sorting

Sorting is the process of arranging data in a specific order (increasing or decreasing). This topic integrates Bubble Sort, Selection Sort, Insertion Sort, Merge Sort, Quick Sort, and Heap Sort.

sort()

  • Works only for list
  • modifies the smallest
  • Sorts in place
  • Use Tim Sort Algorithm

sorted()

  • Works for any iterable
  • Does not modify the passes container, instead returns a list of sorted items

NOTE : Both use Tim Sort Algorithm and Both are Stable

Tim Sort Algorithm

It's a hybrid algorithm that uses 'Merge Sort' and 'Insertion Sort; internally

for sort() & sorted() it uses O(nlog(n)) time

Sort()

Python Implementation 
# List Sort()

l1 = [5, 10, 15, 1]
l1.sort()
print(l1)
Python Implementation 
l2 = [5, 10, 15, 1]
l2.sort(reverse = True)
print(l2)
Python Implementation 
l3 = ['Thrishank', 'Nani', 'Kuntimaddi']
l3.sort()
print(l3)
Python Implementation 
def myfun(s):
  return len(s)

l4 = ['Thrishank', 'Nani', 'Kuntimaddi']
l4.sort(key = myfun)
print(l4)

l4.sort(key = myfun, reverse = True)
print(l4)
Python Implementation 
# Recap for class concept

class Point:
  def __init__(self, x, y):
    self.x = x
    self.y = y

def myFun(p):
  return p.x

l = [Point(1, 15), Point(10, 15), Point(3, 8)]
# print(l)
l.sort(key = myFun)
for i in l:
  print(i.x, i.y)

print()

print(l) # Returns a list of Objects
Python Implementation 
class Point:
  def __init__(self, x, y):
    self.x = x
    self.y = y

  def __lt__(self, other):   # Less than Operation
    return self.x < other.x

l = [Point(1, 15), Point(10, 15), Point(3, 8)]
l.sort()
for i in l:
  print(i.x, i.y)
Python Implementation 
# If both are equal then we compare y values

class Point:
  def __init__(self, x, y):
    self.x = x
    self.y = y

  def __lt__(self, other):   # Less than Operation
    if self.x == other.x:
      return self.y < other.y
    else:
      return self.x < other.x

l = [Point(1, 15), Point(10, 15), Point(3, 8)]
l.sort()
for i in l:
  print(i.x, i.y)

Sorted()

  • Works for any Iterable (List, Tuple, Dictionary, Strings, Set)
  • Returns a list with sorted items
  • Parameters like reverse and key work same way as sort()
Python Implementation 
l = [30, 8, 23, 76, 12, 1, 12]
ls = sorted(l)
print(ls)
Python Implementation 
l = [10, -15, -2, 1]
ls = sorted(l, key = abs)
print(ls)

print()

ls = sorted(l, key = abs, reverse = True)
print(ls)

print()

ls = sorted(l)
print(ls)
Python Implementation 
t = (10, 12, 5, 1)
print(sorted(t))

print()

s = {'Thrishank', 'Nani', 'Kuntimaddi'}
print(sorted(s))

print()

s = 'gfg'
print(sorted(s))

print()

d = { 10 : 'Thrishank', 12 : 'Nani', 5 : 'Kuntimaddi'}
print(sorted(d))

print()

l = [(10, 15), (12, 15), (3, 8)]
print(sorted(l))

Stability in Sorting Algorithms

arr[] = [ ('Anil', 50), ('Ayan', 80), ('Pyush', 50), ('Ramesh', 80) ]

If two items have same value, then they should appear in the same order as they appeared in the original array is called stability.

stable output : [ ('Anil', 50), ('Pyush', 50), ('Ayan', 80), ('Ramesh', 80) ]

unstable output : [ ('Pyush', 50), ('Anil', 50), ('Ayan', 80), ('Ramesh', 80) ]

Stable Sorts

  • Bubble Sort
  • Insertion Sort
  • Merge Sort

Unstable Sorts

  • Selection Sort
  • Quick Sort
  • Heap Sort

Bubble Sort

Applications - Generally preferred in academics

Python Implementation
Theta(n^2)
# Naive Approach

'''
1. Compare each pair of adjacent items in the list.

2. Swap them if the first item is larger than the second.

3. Repeat until you pass through the list without any swaps.

4. The list is sorted when no more swaps are needed.

'''


def bubbleSort(l):
  n = len(l)
  for i in range(n):
    for j in range(n - i - 1):
      if l[j] > l[j + 1]:
        l[j], l[j + 1] = l[j + 1], l[j]

l = [10, 12, 5, 1, 10, 4, 56, 100]
bubbleSort(l)
print(l)
Python Implementation
Theta(n^2)
# Optimized Approach

def bubblesort(l):
  n = len(l)
  for i in range(n-1):
    swapped = False
    for j in range(n - i - 1):
      if l[j] > l[j + 1]:
        l[j], l[j + 1] = l[j + 1], l[j]
        swapped = True
    if swapped == False:
      return

l = [10, 12, 5, 1, 10, 4, 56, 100]
bubblesort(l)
print(l)

Selection Sort

  • Theta(n^2)
  • Does less memory writer comapred to Quick sort, Merge Sort, Insertion Sort. But Cycle sort is optimal in terms of memory
  • Basic idea for Heap Sort
  • Not Stable
  • In Place sort : No need for extra array or space
Python Implementation
Theta(1)
# Approach

def selectionSort(l):
  n = len(l)

  for i in range(n):
    min_index = i
    for j in range(i+1, n):
      if l[j] < l[min_index]:
        min_index = j
    l[i], l[min_index] = l[min_index], l[i]

l = [10, 12, 5, 1, 10, 4, 56, 100]
selectionSort(l)
print(l)

Insertion Sort

  • O(n^2) : Worst Case
  • In-place and stable
  • used in practice for small arrays (Tim Sort & Intro Sort)
  • O(n) : Best Case
Python Implementation
O(n) : Best Case
# Approach

def insertionSort(l):
  for i in range(1, len(l)):
    x = l[i]
    j = i - 1
    while j >= 0 and x < l[i]:
      l[j + 1] = l[j]
      j -= 1
    l[j + 1] = x

l = [10, 12, 5, 1, 10, 4, 56, 100]
insertionSort(l)
print(l)

#                   O(n^2) : Worst Case
  • Divide and Conquer Algorithm (Divide, Conquer, Merge)
  • Stable
  • Time complexity : Theta(n*log(n))
  • Auxiliary Space : O(n)
  • well suited for LinkedList that works in O(1) Auxiliary Space
  • Used in external Sorting

Merge Sort for Two Sorted Arrays

Case Example

Input: a = [10, 15, 20] ; b = [5, 6, 6, 30]
Output: res = [5, 6, 6, 10, 15, 20, 30]

Python Implementation
O(n log(n)) O(n)
# Naive Solution

def merge(a, b):
  res = a + b
  res.sort()
  return res

a = [10, 15, 20]
b = [5, 6, 6, 30]
print(merge(a, b))
Python Implementation
O(n) or O(m+n) O(n)
# Efficient Solution

def mergeList(a, b):
  res = []
  m = len(a)
  n = len(b)
  i = 0
  j = 0

  while i < m and j < n:
    if a[i] < b[j]:
      res.append(a[i])
      i += 1
    else:
      res.append(b[j])
      j += 1

  while i < m:
    res.append(a[i])
    i += 1

  while j < n:
    res.append(b[j])
    j += 1

  return res

l = [10, 15, 20]
r = [5, 6, 6, 30]
print(mergeList(l, r))

Merge Subarray

Case Example

Input:

a = [10, 15, 20, 11, 13]

low = 0

high = 4

mid = 2

Case Example

Output:

a = [10, 11, 13, 15, 20]

so that the idea is split main array into two parts then merge them

Python Implementation
Theta(m+n)
def merge(a, low, mid, high):
  left = a[low : mid + 1]
  right = a[mid + 1 : high + 1]
  i = 0
  j = 0
  k = low

  while i < len(left) and j < len(right):
    if left[i] < right[j]:
      a[k] = left[i]
      k += 1
      i += 1
    else:
      a[k] = right[j]
      j += 1
      k += 1

  while i < len(left):
    a[k] = left[i]
    i += 1
    k += 1

  while j < len(right):
    a[k] = right[j]
    j += 1
    k += 1

a = [10, 15, 20, 11, 13]
merge(a, 0, 2, 4)
print(a)

Merge Sort Algorithm

Python Implementation
Theta(n*log(n)) O(n)
def mergeSort(arr, l, r):
  if r > l:
    mid = (l + r) // 2
    mergeSort(arr, l, mid)
    mergeSort(arr, mid + 1, r)
    merge(arr, l, mid, r)

arr = [10, 15, 20, 11, 13]
mergeSort(arr, 0, 4)
print(arr)

Union of two sorted array

Case Example

Input:

a = [3, 5, 8]

b = [2, 8, 9, 10, 15]

Case Example

Output: 2 3 5 8 9 10 15

Naive Solution

1. Concatenate both lists

2. Sort the concatenated list

3. Print Distinct Elements

Time Complexity : O((m + n) * log(m + n))

m - len of a

n - len of b

Python Implementation 
# Naive Approach

def Print(a, b):
  c = a + b
  c.sort()
  for i in range(0, len(c)):
    if i == 0 or c[i] != c[i - 1]:
      print(c[i], end = " ")

a = [3, 5, 8, 9, 10, 15]
b = [2, 8, 9, 10, 15]
Print(a, b)
Python Implementation
Theta(m+n) Theta(1)
# Efficient Approach

# First, How does the Idea Works..?
'''
a = [_, _, _, i, _, _, _, _, _]
b = [_ ,_, _, _, j, _, _, _, _]

if a[i] == a[i-1]:
  i = i + 1

if b[j] == b[j-1]:
  j = j + 1

if a[i] < b[j]:
  print(a[i], end = " ")
  i = i + 1

if a[i] > b[j]:
  print(b[j], end = " ")
  j = j + 1

if a[i] == b[j]:
  print(a[i], end = " ")
  i = i + 1
  j = j + 1

'''

def PrintUnion(a, b):
  i = 0
  j = 0
  while i < len(a) and j < len(b):
    if i > 0 and a[i] == a[i - 1]:
      i = i + 1
    elif j > 0 and b[j] == b[j-1]:
      j = j + 1
    elif a[i] < b[j]:
      print(a[i], end = " ")
      i = i + 1
    elif a[i] > b[j]:
      print(b[j], end = " ")
      j = j + 1
    else:                          # a[i] == b[j]
      print(a[i], end = " ")
      i = i + 1
      j = j + 1

  while i < len(a):
      if i > 0 and a[i] == a[i-1]:
        print(a[i], end = " ")
        i = i + 1

  while j < len(b):
      if j > 0 and b[j] == b[j-1]:
        print(b[j], end = " ")
        j = j + 1

a = [3, 5, 8, 9, 10, 15]
b = [2, 8, 9, 10, 15]
PrintUnion(a, b)

Intersection of Two sorted Array

Case Example

Input:

a[] = { 3, 5, 10, 10, 10, 10, 15, 15, 20 }

b[] = { 5, 10, 10, 15, 30 }

Case Example

Output: 5, 10, 15

Python Implementation
O(m+n) O(1)
# Naive

def intersection(a, b, m, n):
  for i in range(m):
    if i > 0 and a[i-1] == a[i]:
      continue
    else:
      for j in range(n):
        if a[i] == b[j]:
          print(a[i], end = " ")
          break

a = [3, 5, 10, 10, 10, 10, 15, 15, 20 ]
b = [ 5, 10, 10, 15, 30 ]

intersection(a, b, 9, 5)
Python Implementation
O(m+n) O(1)
# Efficient

# First, How does the idea works..?
'''
a = [_, _, _, i, _, _, _, _, _]
b = [_ ,_, _, _, j, _, _, _, _]

if i > 0 && a[i] == a[i-1]:
  i += 1
  continue

if a[i] < b[j]:
  i += 1

if a[i] > b[j]:
  j += 1

if a[i] == b[j]:
  print(a[i], end = " ")
  i += 1
  j += 1

'''

def intersection(a, b, m, n):
  i = 0
  j = 0

  while i < m and j < n:
    if i > 0 and a[i-1] == a[i]:  # Don't consider common elements in Set A
      i += 1
      continue
    elif a[i] < b[j]: # Moves i value based on j value
      i += 1
    elif a[i] > b[j]: # Moves j value based on i value
      j += 1
    elif a[i] == b[j]:
      print(a[i], end = " ")
      i += 1
      j += 1

a = [3, 5, 10, 10, 10, 10, 15, 15, 20 ]
b = [ 5, 10, 10, 15, 30 ]
intersection(a, b, len(a), len(b))

Count inversions in Array

Inversions : A pair arr[i], arr[j] forms an inversion when ( i < j ) and arr[i] > arr[j]

Case Example

I/P = [2, 4, 1, 3, 5]
O/P = 3

Python Implementation
O(n^2) O(1)
# Naive

def inversion(arr):
  n = len(arr)
  res = 0
  for i in range(n-1):
    for j in range(i+1, n):
      if arr[i] > arr[j]:
        res += 1
  return res

arr = [2, 4, 1, 3, 5, 2]
print(inversion(arr))
Python Implementation
O(n*log(n)) O(n)
# Efficient
'''

arr[] = [_, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _]

         l                 m  m+1                        r

m = (l+r)/2

Every Inversion(x, y) where x > y has three possibilities

a) Both x & y lie in the first half
b) Both x & y lie in the second half
c) x lies in the first half and y lies in the second half

'''

def countInv(arr, l, r):
  res = 0
  if l < r:
    mid = (l + r) // 2
    res += countInv(arr, l, mid)
    res += countInv(arr, mid+1, r)
    res += countMerge(arr, l, mid, r)
  return res

def countMerge(arr, l, mid, r):
  left = arr[l : mid+1]
  right = arr[mid+1 : r+1]
  res, i, j, k = 0, 0, 0, l
  while i < len(left) and j < len(right):
    if left[i] <= right[j]:
      arr[k] = left[i]
      i += 1
    else:
      arr[k] = right[j]
      j += 1
      res += (len(left) - i)
    k += 1

  while i < len(left):
    arr[k] = left[i]
    i += 1
    k += 1

  while j < len(right):
    arr[k] = right[j]
    j += 1
    k += 1

  return res

arr = [2, 4, 1, 3, 5]
print(countInv(arr, 0, 4))
Case Example

Input: l = [3, 8, 6, 12, 10, 7]

p = 5

Case Example

Output:

l = [3, 6, 7, 8, 12, 10]

or

l = [6, 3, 7, 12, 8, 10]

............

Python Implementation
Theta(n) Theta(n)
# Naive Solution

def partition(arr, p):
  n = len(arr)
  arr[p], arr[n-1] = arr[n-1], arr[p]
  temp = []

  for x in arr:
    if x <= arr[n-1]:
      temp.append(x)

  for x in arr:
    if x > arr[n-1]:
      temp.append(x)

  for i in range(len(temp)):
    arr[i] = temp[i]

  return temp

l = [3, 8, 6, 12, 10, 7]
p = 3                        # Index Value to swap with last element
print(partition(l, p))

Lomuto Parition (Guarantee)

arr = [10, 80, 30, 90, 40, 50, 70]

l = 0 h = 6

pivot = 70 # Last Element

Python Implementation 
def lomutoPartition(arr, l, h):
  pivot = arr[h]
  i = l - 1

  for j in range(l, h):
    if arr[j] <= pivot:
      i += 1
      arr[i], arr[j] = arr[j], arr[i]

  arr[i+1], arr[h] = arr[h], arr[i+1]
  return i+1

arr = [10, 80, 30, 90, 40, 50, 70]
print(arr)
n = len(arr)
print(lomutoPartition(arr, 0, n-1))
print(arr)

Hoare's Partition (No Guarantee)

rr = [10, 80, 30, 90, 40, 50, 70]

l = 0 h = 6

after partition, pivot can be anywhere in leftside

Python Implementation 
def hoarePartition(arr, l, h):
  pivot = arr[l]
  i = l - 1
  j = h + 1

  while True:
    i += 1
    while arr[i] < pivot:
      i += 1
    j = j - 1
    while arr[j] > pivot:
      j -= 1
    if i >= j:
      return j
    arr[i], arr[j] = arr[j], arr[i]

arr = [10, 80, 30, 90, 40, 50, 70, 100, 120, 10, 5]
print(arr)
n = len(arr)
print(hoarePartition(arr, 0, n-1))
print(arr)

Quick Sort

1. Divide and Conquer Algorithm

2. Worst Time Case : O(n^2)

3. Despite O(n^2) worst cae, it is considered faster because of the following

  • In-Place
  • cache friendly
  • Average-case : O(n * logn)
  • Tail Recursive

4. Partition is key function (Naive, Lomuto, Hoarse)

where Naive is stable and Lomuto, Hoarse are unstable

Python Implementation 
# Using Lomuto

def qsort(arr, l, h):
  if l < h:
    p = lomutoPartition(arr, l, h)
    qsort(arr, l, p-1)
    qsort(arr, p+1, h)

arr = [10, 80, 30, 90, 40, 50, 70]
print(arr)
n = len(arr)
qsort(arr, 0, n-1)
print(arr)

# Best case : Theta(log n)

'''
Dry Run

1. Based on the pivot value it sort the elements

2. [3, 5, 6, 7, 1, 2, 4]

  - pivot is 4

  - Element on left side are less than 4

  - Element on right side are greater than 4

3. sorted array : [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7]

'''
Python Implementation 
# Using Hoarse

def qsort(arr, l, h):
  if l < h:
    p = hoarePartition(arr, l, h)
    qsort(arr, l, p)
    qsort(arr, p+1, h)

arr = [10, 80, 30, 90, 40, 50, 70]
print(arr)
n = len(arr)
qsort(arr, 0, n-1)
print(arr)

# Note : Hoare's Partition is three times faster than Lomuto Partition

'''
1. Firstly this the collection is completely divided into single cell

2. Then it is conquered in a way they swapped in acesending order

'''

Analysis of QuickSort : Hoare's Partition

Best Case : Theta(n * log_2 n)

Worst Case : Theta(n^2)

Average Case : O(n * log n)

Space Complexity : Theta(n)

  • can be seen as an optimization over selection sort
  • Two steps
  • build a maxHeap
  • Repeatedly swap root with last node, reduce heap size by 1, and heapify
  • Time complexity : O(n * logn)
  • Auxiliary Space : O(1)
  • Not stable
  • used in Hybrid sorting algorithms like Intro Sort
Python Implementation 
# Step 1 : Build a maxHeap

def maxheapify(arr, n, i):
    largest = i
    l = 2 * i + 1
    r = 2 * i + 2

    if l < n and arr[i] < arr[l]:
        largest = l

    if r < n and arr[largest] < arr[r]:
        largest = r

    if largest != i:
        arr[i], arr[largest] = arr[largest], arr[i]
        maxheapify(arr, n, largest)

def buildHeap(arr):                        # Build Heap Tree from List
    n = len(arr)
    for i in range(n//2, -1, -1):
        maxheapify(arr, n, i)

arr = [50, 20, 10, 4, 15]
buildHeap(arr)
print(arr)
Python Implementation 
# Heap Sort

def heapsprt(arr):
    n = len(arr)
    buildHeap(arr)

    for i in range(n-1, 0, -1):
        arr[i], arr[0] = arr[0], arr[i]
        maxheapify(arr, i, 0)

arr = [50, 20, 10, 4, 15]
heapsprt(arr)
print(arr)

# Time Complexity ; O(n * logn)

Hashing

It is used to implement dictionaries and sets

  • Search
  • Insert
  • Delete

These operations can be done in O(1) on Average

NOTE:

Not useful for

  • Finding closest value
  • Sorted Data
  • Prefix Searching (Trie)

Applications

  • Dictionaries
  • Database Indexing
  • Cryptography
  • Caches
  • Symbol Tables in compilers/Interpreter
  • Routers
  • Getting data from databases

Direct Address Table

Imagine a situation where you have 1000 Keys with values from (0 to 999), how would you implement following in O(1) Time.

1. Search

2. Insert

3. Delete

So the problem at certain point araises

It cannot handle large data

Hash Intro

Universe of Keys ---> Hash Function ---> Hash Table

How Hash Function work....?

1. Should always map a large key to same small key

2. should generate values from 0 to m-1

3. should be fast, O(1) for integers and O(len) for string of length 'len'

4. should uniformly distribute large keys into Hash Table slots.

Example:

1. h(large_key) = large_key % m

2. For Strings weighted sum

3. universal Hashing

Collision Handling

  • If we know keys in advance, then we can perfect hashing
  • If we do not know keys, then we will use the following
  • chaining
  • open Addressing
  • Linear Probing
  • Quadratic Probing
  • Double hashing

Chaining

consider the function : hash(key) = key % n

  • where n = size of hash table

Keys = [50, 21, 58, 17, 15, 49, 56, 22, 23, 25]

0 | 21 | --> | 49 | --> | 56 |

1 | 50 | --> | 15 | --> | 22 |

2 | 58 | --> | 23 |

3 | 17 |

4 | 25 |

5 | |

6 | |

Hash Table (Array of Linked List Headers)

Performance of chaining

m = number of slots in Hash Table

n = number of keys to be inserted

Load Factor α = n/m

Expected chain length = α

Expected time to search = O(1 + α)

Expected time to delete = O(1 + α)

Load Factor

Initial Table (10 slots, empty):

[_, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _] Load Factor: 0.0

After 5 insertions:

[x, x, x, x, x, _, _, _, _, _] Load Factor: 0.5

After 10 insertions (fully occupied):

[x, x, x, x, x, x, x, x, x, x] Load Factor: 1.0

After resizing and 11th insertion (20 slots):

[x, x, x, x, x, x, x, x, x, x, x, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _, _] Load Factor: 0.55

Data Structure for storing Chains

  • LinkedList
  • Dynamic sized arrays
  • vector in C++
  • arrayList in Java
  • List in Python
  • self balancing BST (AVL Tree, RedBlock Tree)
Python Implementation 
# Implementation of chaining in Python

# hash(x) = x % bucket

class myHash:
  def __init__(self, b):
    self.Bucket = b
    self.table = [[] for x in range(b)]


  def insert(self, x):
    i = x % self.Bucket
    self.table[i].append(x)


  def delete(self, x):
    i = x % self.Bucket
    self.table[i].remove(x)

  def search(self, x):
    i = x % self.Bucket
    return x in self.table[i]

  def print(self):
    print(self.table)


h = myHash(7)
h.insert(70)
h.insert(71)
h.insert(9)
h.insert(56)
h.insert(72)
h.print()

h.delete(56)
h.print()

print(h.search(9))
print(h.search(93))

Open Addressing

1. number of slots in hash table >= number of keys to be inserted

2. cache friendly

Linear Probing

  • linearly searching for next empty slot when collision comes or occurs

Example:

size of hash table : 7

Keys = [50, 51, 49, 16, 56, 15, 19]

remainder

50 - 1

51 - 2

49 - 0

16 - 2

56 - 0

15 - 1

19 - 5

Hash Table

0 | 49 |

1 | 50 |

2 | 51 |

3 | 16 |

4 | 56 |

5 | 15 |

6 | 19 |

How to Handle deletions in open addressing...?

-> problem with simply making slot empty when we delete

-> mark as deleted so that when searching if it saw deleted then it will continue

How to Handle searching in open addressing...?

-> we compute hash()

-> we got to that index and compare

-> if found return true

else:

search linearly

-> we stop when it reaches to same index

clustering

clustering refers to the phenomenon where multiple elements are hashed to the same or nearby slots, leading to groups or "clusters" of occupied slots. This clustering can significantly degrade the performance of the hash table, particularly for operations like insertion, search, and deletion.

How to Handle clustering problem with linear probing...?

hash(key, i) = (h(key) + i) % 7

where h(key) = key % 7

1. Quadratic probing load factor : (α < 0.5) || n/m where m is prime

hash(key, i) = (h(key) + l^2) % m

2. Double Hashing

hash(key, i) = (h1(key) + i * h2(key)) % m

  • 2.1 : if h2(key) is relatively prime to m, then it always find a free slot if there is one.
  • 2.2 : Distribute keys more uniformly than linear probing and quadratic hashing
  • 2.3 : no clustering

Algorithm for DoubleHashingInsert

void doubleHashingInsert(key) {

if (table is full)

return error;

probe = h1(key), offset = h2(key);

while (table[probe] is occupied)

probe = (probe + offset) % m;

table[probe] = key;

}

Performace Analysis of Search

α = n/m (should be <= 1)

Assumption : Every probe sequence looks at a random location

(1 - α) fraction of table is empty

expected number of probe required= ...? (Initally can't say)

Frequencies of array elements

Case Example

Input: arr[] = [10, 12, 10, 15, 10, 20, 12, 12]
Output: 10 3; 12 3; 15 1; 20 1

Python Implementation
O(n^2)
# Naive Approach

def countFreq(arr, n):

  for i in range(n):
    flag = False

    for j in range(i):
      if arr[i] == arr[j]:
        flag = True
        break

    if flag == True:
      continue
    count = 1

    for j in range(i+1, n):
      if arr[i] == arr[j]:
        count += 1

    print(arr[i], count)


arr = [10, 12, 10, 15, 10, 20, 12, 12]
n = len(arr)
countFreq(arr, n)


# Auxiliary Space : O(1)
Python Implementation
O(n)
# Efficient Solution

def countFreq(arr, n):

  hmp = {}

  for i in range(n):
    if arr[i] in hmp.keys():
      hmp[arr[i]] += 1
    else:
      hmp[arr[i]] = 1

  for x in hmp:
    print(x, hmp[x])

arr = [10, 12, 10, 15, 10, 20, 12, 12]
n = len(arr)
countFreq(arr, n)

# Auxiliary Space : O(n)

Implementation of Open Addressing

Python Implementation 
class myHash:
  def __init__(self, c):
    self.capacity = c
    self.table = [None] * c
    self.size = 0

  def hash(self, key):
    return key % self.capacity

  def search(self, key):
    h = self.hash(key)
    i = h
    while self.table[i] != None:
      if self.table[i] == key:
        return True
      i = (i + 1) % self.capacity
      if i == h:
        return False
    return False

  def insert(self, key):
    if self.size == self.capacity:
      return False
    if self.search(key) == True:
      return False
    h = self.hash(key)
    i = h
    while self.table[i] != None and self.table[i] != -1:
      i = (i + 1) % self.capacity
      if i == h:
        return False
    self.table[i] = key
    self.size += 1
    return True

  def delete(self, key):
    h = self.hash(key)
    i = h
    while self.table[i] != None:
      if self.table[i] == key:
        self.table[i] = -1
        self.size -= 1
        return True
      i = (i + 1) % self.capacity
      if i == h:
        return False
    return False

  def printh(self):
    print(self.table)

obj = myHash(7)
obj.insert(70)
obj.insert(71)
obj.insert(9)
obj.insert(56)
obj.insert(72)
obj.search(9)
obj.delete(9)
obj.search(9)
obj.search(56)
obj.insert(56)
obj.printh()

Chaining

1. Hash Table never Fails

2. Less senstive to Hash Functions

3. Poor cache Performance

4. Extra space for Links

--> (1+α)

Open Addressing

1. Table may become full and resizing becomes mandatory

2. Extra care required for clustering

3. Cache Friendly

4. Extra space might be needed to achieve same performance all changing

--> 1/(1-α)

Set

1. Distinct Elements

2. Unordered

3. No Indexing

4. Union, Intersection, set-diff are fast

5. User Hashing Internally

Check for Rotation

Case Example

Input: s1 = "ABCD"

s2 = "CDAB"

Case Example

Output: Yes

Explaination :

ABCD --> BCDA --> CDAB

Python Implementation
O(n) O(n)
# Solution

def areRotations(s1, s2):
  if len(s1) != len(s2):
    return False
  temp = ''
  temp = s1 + s1

  return temp.find(s2) != -1

print(areRotations("ABCD", "CDAB"))

print(areRotations("ABCD", "ABCA"))

Check if a string is Subsequence

Case Example

Input: s1 = 'ABCD'; s2 = 'AD'
Output: True
Input: s1 = "ABCDE"; s2 = "AED"
Output: False

Python Implementation 
# Naive

def issubseq(s1, s2):
  i, j = 0, 0

  while i < len(s1) and j < len(s2):
    if s1[i] == s2[j]:
      j += 1
    i += 1
  return j == len(s2)

print(issubseq("ABCD", "AD"))
print(issubseq("ABCDE", "AED"))
Python Implementation 
# Recursion

def issubseq(s1, s2, m, n):
  if n == 0:
    return True
  if m == 0:
    return False
  if s1[m-1] == s2[n-1]:
    return issubseq(s1, s2, m-1, n-1)
  return issubseq(s1, s2, m-1, n)

print(issubseq("ABCD", "AD", 4, 2))
print(issubseq("ABCDE", "AED", 5, 3))

Check for Anagram

Anagram : length & character & count should be same

Case Example

Input: s1 = 'listen'

s2 = 'silent'

Case Example

Output: Yes

---------------------

Case Example

Input: s1 = "aabc"

s2 = "acab"

Case Example

Output: Yes

Python Implementation
O(n*logn) O(n)
# Naive

def anagram(s1, s2):
  if len(s1) != len(s2):
    return False
  s1 = sorted(s1)
  s2 = sorted(s2)

  return s1 == s2

s1 = "listen"
s2 = "silent"

print(anagram(s1, s2))

s1 = "aabd"
s2 = "acab"

print(anagram(s1, s2))
Python Implementation
O(n) O(1)
# Efficient

def anagram(s1, s2):
  if len(s1) != len(s2):
    return False
  count = [0] * 256

  for i in range(len(s1)):
    count[ord(s1[i])] += 1
    count[ord(s2[i])] -= 1
  for i in range(256):
    if count[i] != 0:
      return False
  return True

s1 = "listen"
s2 = "silent"

print(anagram(s1, s2))

s1 = "aabd"
s2 = "acab"

print(anagram(s1, s2))

Leftmost Repeating Character

Case Example

Input: str = "geeksforgeeks"
Output: 0
Input: str = "abbcc"
Output: 1
Input: str = "abcd"
Output: -1

Python Implementation
O(n^2) O(1)
# Naive Approach

def leftmost(str):
  for i in range(len(str)):
    for j in range(i+1, len(str)):
      if str[i] == str[j]:
        return i
  return -1

str = "geeksforgeeks"

print(leftmost(str))
Python Implementation
O(n) O(1)
# Better Approach

CHAR = 256

def leftmost(str):
  count = [0] * CHAR

  for i in range(len(str)):
    count[ord(str[i])] += 1

  for i in range(len(str)):
    if count[ord(str[i])] > 1:
      return i

  return -1

str = "geeksforgeeks"

print(leftmost(str))
Python Implementation
O(n) O(1)
# Method 1

import sys

CHAR = 256

def leftmost(str):

  fIndex = [-1] * CHAR
  res = sys.maxsize

  for i in range(len(str)):

    if fIndex[ord(str[i])] == -1:

      fIndex[ord(str[i])] = i

    else:

      res = min(res, fIndex[ord(str[i])])

  return res

str = "Thrishank"

print(leftmost(str))
Python Implementation
O(n) O(1)
# Method 2

CHAR = 256

def leftmost(str):
  visited = [False] * CHAR
  res = -1

  for i in range(len(str) -1, -1, -1):
    if visited[ord(str[i])] == True:
      res = i
    else:
      visited[ord(str[i])] = True
  return res

str = "Thrishank"

print(leftmost(str))

Leftmost Non-Repeating Character

Input: str = 'geeksforgeeks'
Output: 5
Input: str = 'abcabc'
Output: -1

O(n^2) O(1)
# Naive

def nonRep(str):
  for i in range(len(str)):
    flag = False

    for j in range(i+1, len(str)):
      if str[i] == str[j]:
        flag = True
        break

    if flag == False:
      return i
    return -1

str1 = "aaa"

print(nonRep(str1))

str1 = "geeksforgeeks"

print(nonRep(str1))
Python Implementation
O(n) O(CHAR)
# Better Solution

CHAR = 256

def nonRep(str):
  count = [0] * CHAR

  for i in range(len(str)):
    count[ord(str[i])] += 1

  for i in range(len(str)):
    if count[ord(str[i])] == 1:
      return i
  return -1

str1 = "aaa"

print(nonRep(str1))

str1 = "geeksforgeeks"

print(nonRep(str1))
Python Implementation
O(n) O(CHAR)
# Method 1

import sys

CHAR = 256

def nonRep(str):

  fIndex = [-1] * CHAR
  res = sys.maxsize

  for i in range(len(str)):
    if fIndex[ord(str[i])] == -1:
      fIndex[ord(str[i])] = i
    else:
      fIndex[ord(str[i])] = -2

  for i in range(CHAR):
    if fIndex[i] >= 0:
      res = min(res, fIndex[i])

  return res if res != sys.maxsize else -1



str1 = "aaa"
print(nonRep(str1))

str1 = "geeksforgeeks"
print(nonRep(str1))

Reverse Words in a String

Case Example

Input: str = "Welcome to gfg"
Output: "gfg to Welcome"
Input: str = 'abc'
Output: abc

Python Implementation
O(n)
# Naive Solution

'''
1. Initialize an empty stack
2. Split the string into words
3. Push each word onto the stack
4. Initialize the output string
5. Pop words from the stack and append them to the output string
6. Remove the trailing space and return the reversed string

'''

def reverseWordsUsingStack(str):
    stack = []
    words = str.split()

    for word in words:
        stack.append(word)

    reversed_str = ""

    while stack:
        reversed_str += stack.pop() + " "

    return reversed_str.strip()

str = "Welcome to gfg"
result = reverseWordsUsingStack(str)
print(result)

# Time Comeplexity : O(n)
Python Implementation
O(n) O(1)
# Idea for O(1) Auxiliary Space Solution

'''
1. Reverse the individual word
2. Reverse the whole string
'''

def reverse(s, b, e):
  while b < e:
    s[b], s[e] = s[e], s[b]
    b += 1
    e -= 1

def reversewords(s):
  n = len(s)
  b = 0
  for e in range(n):
    if s[e] == ' ':
      reverse(s, b, e-1)
      b = e + 1
  reverse(s, b, n-1)
  reverse(s, 0, n-1)

s = list("Welcome to gfg")
reversewords(s)
print("".join(s))

Singly Linked List

A linked list is a linear data structure where elements are not stored in contiguous memory locations. Instead, elements (nodes) are linked together using pointers.

Definition

A Singly Linked List consists of a sequence of nodes, where each node contains a Data field and a Next reference pointer pointing to the next node in the list. The list ends when the next pointer points to None.

Structure of a Node

In Python, a node is implemented using a class container that holds data and the next address reference.

Python Node Class 
class Node:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data
        self.next = None

Core Operations on Singly Linked List

1. Insertion at Beginning

Insert a node at the head of the list. This operation is highly efficient because it does not require traversing the list.

Python
O(1) O(1) 
def insert_at_beginning(head, data):
    new_node = Node(data)
    new_node.next = head
    return new_node

2. Insertion at End

Insert a node at the tail of the list. If we don't have a tail pointer, this requires traversing the entire list first.

Python
O(n) O(1) 
def insert_at_end(head, data):
    new_node = Node(data)
    if head is None:
        return new_node
    current = head
    while current.next is not None:
        current = current.next
    current.next = new_node
    return head

3. Deletion of Head Node

Delete the first node from the list and return the new head node reference.

Python
O(1) O(1) 
def delete_head(head):
    if head is None:
        return None
    return head.next

4. Search in Linked List

Find if a target element is present in the list. Returns the position (1-indexed) or -1.

Python
O(n) O(1) 
def search_list(head, target):
    current = head
    position = 1
    while current is not None:
        if current.data == target:
            return position
        current = current.next
        position += 1
    return -1

Comparison: Array vs. Linked List

Operation Array / List (Contiguous) Linked List (Pointers)
Access by index O(1) (Constant time lookup) O(n) (Requires sequential scan)
Insert at start O(n) (Requires shifting elements) O(1) (Just re-link nodes)
Insert at end O(1) amortized (Dynamic arrays) O(n) (or O(1) if tail kept)
Extra Memory None (contiguous block) Next pointer for every element node

Circular Linked List

10 --> 5 --> 20 --> 15 --> goes to first element

Python Implementation 
class Node:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data
        self.next = None

head = Node(10)
head.next = Node(5)
head.next.next = Node(20)
head.next.next.next = Node(15)
head.next.next.next.next = head

temp = head
while temp.next != head:
    print(temp.data)
    temp = temp.next
print(temp.data)

Advantages

1. We can traverse the whole list from any node

2. Implementation of Algorithms like Round Robin

3. We can insert at the beginning and end by just maintaining one tail reference/pointer

Disadvantages

1. Implementation of operations becomes complex

Traversing

Python Implementation 
# Implementation

def printCircular(head):
  if head == None:
    return

  print(head.data, end = " ")
  curr = head.next
  while curr != head:
    print(curr.data, end=" ")
    curr = curr.next

head = Node(10)
head.next = Node(5)
head.next.next = Node(20)
head.next.next.next = Node(15)
head.next.next.next.next = head

printCircular(head)

Insert at beginning

Python Implementation 
# Method 1 (Linear Time)

def insertBag(head, x):
  temp = Node(x)
  if head == None:
    temp.next = temp
    return temp
  curr = head
  while curr.next != head:
    curr = curr.next
  curr.next = temp
  temp.next = head
  return temp

head = insertBag(head, 25)
printCircular(head)
Python Implementation 
# Method 2 (Constant Time)

def insertBag(head, x):
  temp = Node(x)
  if head == None:
    temp.next = temp
    return temp
  temp.next = head.next
  head.next = temp
  head.data, temp.data = temp.data, head.data
  return head

head = insertBag(head, 30)
printCircular(head)

Insert at End

Python Implementation 
# Naive Solution

def insertEnd(head, x):
  temp = Node(x)
  if head == None:
    temp.next = temp
    return temp
  curr = head
  while curr.next != head:
    curr = curr.next
  curr.next = temp
  temp.next = head
  return head

head = insertEnd(head, 35)
printCircular(head)
Python Implementation 
# Efficient Solution

def insertEnd(head, x):
  temp = Node(x)
  if head == None:
    temp.next = temp
    return temp
  temp.next = head.next
  head.next = temp
  head.data, temp.data = temp.data, head.data
  return temp

head = insertEnd(head, 40)
printCircular(head)

Delete head

Python Implementation 
# Naive Solution

def delHead(head):
  if head == None:
    return None
  elif head.next == head:
    return None
  curr = head
  while curr.next != head:
    curr = curr.next

  curr.next = head.next
  return curr.next

head = delHead(head)
printCircular(head)
Python Implementation 
# Efficient Solution

def delHead(head):
  if head == None:
    return None
  elif head.next == head:
    return None
  head.data = head.next.data
  head.next = head.next.next
  return head

head = delHead(head)
printCircular(head)

Delete kth Node

Python Implementation 
def delkthNode(head, k):
  if head == None:
    return None

  elif k == 1:
    return delHead(head)

  curr = head
  for i in range(1, k-2):
    curr = curr.next
  curr.next = curr.next.next
  return head

head = delkthNode(head, 2)
printCircular(head)

Doubly Linked List

None <--> 10 <--> 20 <--> 30 <--> None

Python Implementation 
class Node:
  def __init__(self, data):
    self.data = data
    self.prev = None
    self.next = None

head = Node(10)
temp1 = Node(20)
temp2 = Node(30)

head.next = temp1
temp1.prev = head

temp1.next = temp2
temp2.prev = temp1

temp = head
while temp is not None:
  print(temp.data)
  temp = temp.next

Advantages

1. can be traversed in both directions

2. A given delete a node in O(1) time with given reference/pointer to it

3. Insert/Delete before a given node

4. Insert/Delete from both ends in O(1) time by maintaining tail

Disadvantages

1. Extra Space for prev

2. Code becomes more complex

Insert at beginning

Python Implementation
O(1)
# Insert at beginning

class Node:
  def __init__(self, data):
    self.data = data
    self.prev = None
    self.next = None

def printList(head):
    temp = head
    while temp is not None:
      print(temp.data, end = ' ')
      temp = temp.next

def insertAtBeginning(head, data):
    temp = Node(data)
    temp.next = head
    head.prev = temp
    return temp

head = Node(10)
temp1 = Node(20)
temp2 = Node(30)

head.next = temp1
temp1.prev = head

temp1.next = temp2
temp2.prev = temp1

head = insertAtBeginning(head, 5)

printList(head)

Insert at the End

Python Implementation 
def insertEnd(head, x):
  temp = Node(x)
  if head == None:
    return temp

  curr = head
  while curr.next is not None:
    curr = curr.next

  curr.next = temp
  temp.prev = curr

  return head

head = insertEnd(head, 40)
printList(head)

Delete head

Python Implementation 
def delHead(head):
  if head == None:
    return None

  if head.next == None:
    return None

  head = head.next
  head.prev = None
  return head

head = delHead(head)
printList(head)

Delete last Node

Python Implementation 
def delEnd(head):
  if head == None:
    return None

  if head.next == None:
    return None

  curr = head
  while curr.next.next is not None:
    curr = curr.next

  curr.next = None
  return head

head = delEnd(head)
printList(head)

Reverse a Double Linked List

Python Implementation 
def reverseDLL(head):
  if head == None:
    return None

  if head.next == None:
    return head

  curr = head
  prev = None

  while curr != None:
    prev = curr
    curr.next, curr.prev = curr.prev, curr.next
    curr = curr.prev

  return prev

head = reverseDLL(head)
printList(head)

Stack

10 | 20 | 30 | 40 | --> push(x) | pop()

Last IN First OUT

Operations

isEmpty() : if stack is empty returns True

push(x) : Insert an item to the top of Stack

pop() : Removes an item from the top

peek() : Returns top item

size() : Returns the size

UnderFlow : When pop() and peek() called no empty stack

OverFlow : when push() called on a Full Stack

Stack in Python

1. Using List

2. Using collection.deque

3. Using queue.LIFOQueue (Advanced Topic)

4. Using our own Implementation

Python Implementation 
# Method 1 : Using List

stack = []

stack.append('a')
stack.append('b')
stack.append('c')

print('Initial Stack')
print(stack)

print('\nElements poped from stack')
print(stack.pop())
print(stack.pop())
print(stack.pop())

print('\nStack after elements are poped')
print(stack)

stack = deque()
stack.append('a')

print(stack.pop())
print(stack.pop())

top = stack[-1]
print(top)

print(len(stack))
Python Implementation
O(1)
# Linked List Implementation

class Node:
  def __init__(self, value):
    self.value = value
    self.next = None

class myStack:
  def __init__(self):
    self.head = None
    self.size = 0

  def push(self, value):
    node = Node(value)
    if self.head:
      node.next = self.head
    self.head = node
    self.size += 1

  def pop(self):
    if self.head:
      temp = self.head
      self.head = self.head.next
      self.size -= 1
      return temp.value
    else:
      return None

  def peek(self):
    if self.head:
      return self.head.value
    else:
      return None

  def sizes(self):
    return self.size

def printList(head):
  temp = head
  while temp:
    print(temp.value, end = " ")
    temp = temp.next

s = myStack()
s.push(1)
s.push(2)
s.push(3)
printList(s.head)
print()
print(s.sizes())
print(s.pop())
print(s.peek())

Check for Balanced parenthesis

Case Example

Input: s = '([])'
Output: Yes
Input: s = '([)]'
Output: No

Python Implementation
O(n)
def isBalanced(s):
  stack = []
  for x in expr:
    if x in ['(', '{', '[']:
      stack.append(x)
    else:
      if not stack:
        return False
      elif isMarching(stack[-1], x) == False:
        return False
      else:
        stack.pop()
  if not stack:
    return True
  else:
    return False

def isMarching(a, b):
  if a == '(' and b == ')':
    return True
  elif a == '{' and b == '}':
    return True
  elif a == '[' and b == ']':
    return True
  else:
    return False

expr = input()
if isBalanced(expr):
  print('Yes')
else:
  print('No')

# Method : Using Linked List Implementation

class Node:
  def __init__(self, data):
    self.data = data
    self.next = None

class Queue:
  def __init__(self):
    self.head = None
    self.last = None
    self.size = 0

  def size(self):
    return self.size

  def isEmpty(self):
    return self.size == 0

  def getFront(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return None
    return self.head.data

  def getRear(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return None
    return self.last.data

  def enqueue(self, data):
    temp = Node(data)
    if self.isEmpty():
      self.head = temp
      self.last = temp
    else:
      self.last.next = temp
      self.last = temp
    self.size += 1

  def dequeue(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return None
    temp = self.head.data
    self.head = self.head.next
    self.size -= 1
    return temp

q = Queue()
q.enqueue(10)
q.enqueue(20)
q.enqueue(30)

print(q.getFront()) # 10
print(q.getRear()) # 30

q.dequeue()
print(q.getFront()) # 20
print(q.getRear()) # 30

print(q.size) # 2
print(q.isEmpty()) # False

Queue Implementation using Circular List

Python Implementation 
class myQueue:
  def __init__(self, c):
    self.l = [None] * c
    self.cap = c
    self.size = 0
    self.front = 0
    self.rear = 0

# deque()   -> front = (front + 1) % cap
# enqueue() -> rear = (rear + 1) % cap

  def getFront(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return None
    return self.l[self.front]

  def getRear(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return None
    return self.l[self.rear - 1]

  def size(self):
    return self.size

  def isEmpty(self):
    return self.size == 0

  def isFull(self):
    return self.size == self.cap

  def enqueue(self, data):
    if self.isFull():
      return None
    self.l[self.rear] = data
    self.rear = (self.rear + 1) % self.cap
    self.size += 1

  def dequeue(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return None
    temp = self.l[self.front]
    self.front = (self.front + 1) % self.cap
    self.size -= 1

    return temp

def printList(l, cap):
  for i in range(cap):
    print(l[i], end = " ")
  print()

s = myQueue(5)
s.enqueue(10)
s.enqueue(20)
s.enqueue(30)
s.enqueue(40)
s.enqueue(50)

print(s.getFront()) # 10
print(s.getRear()) # 50

s.dequeue()
print(s.getFront()) # 20
print(s.getRear()) # 50

print(s.size) # 4
print(s.isEmpty()) # False
print(s.isFull()) # False

printList(s.l, s.cap) # 20 30 40 50 0

Queue Implementation Stack using Queue

Idea :

Two Queues

q1 = To keep the actual items

q2 = To be used as an auxiliary queue

push(10) : q1 = [10] ; q2 = []

push(20) : q1 = [ ] ; q2 = [10]

q1 = [20] ; q2 = [10]

q1 = [20, 10] ; q2 = [ ]

push(x)

a) Move Everything from q1 to q2

b) Enqueue x to q1

c) Move Everything from q2 to q1

pop() : Remove front of q1

top() : Return front of q1

size() : Return size of q1

isEmpty() : Return q1.isEmpty()

isFull() : Return False

Optimization

push(x) :

a) Enqueue x to q1

b) Move Everything from q1 to q2

c) Swap q1 and q2

Python Implementation 
from collections import deque

class Stack:
    def __init__(self):
        self.q1 = deque()
        self.q2 = deque()

    def push(self, x):
        self.q2.append(x)
        # Move all elements from q1 to q2
        while self.q1:
            self.q2.append(self.q1.popleft())

        # Swap the queues
        self.q1, self.q2 = self.q2, self.q1

    def pop(self):
        if not self.isEmpty():
            return self.q1.popleft()
        return None  # Return None if the stack is empty

    def top(self):
        if not self.isEmpty():
            return self.q1[0]
        return None  # Return None if the stack is empty

    def size(self):
        return len(self.q1)

    def isEmpty(self):
        return len(self.q1) == 0

    def isFull(self):
        return False  # Stack cannot be full since we're using dynamic arrays

# Example usage:
s = Stack()
s.push(10)
s.push(20)
s.push(30)

print(s.top())  # Output: 30
s.pop()
print(s.top())  # Output: 20

Deque

At both the ends we can insert & delete elements

  • InsertFront()
  • DeleteFront()
  • InsertRear()
  • DeleteRear()
  • getFront()
  • getRear()
  • isFull()
  • isEmpty()
  • size()

Applications

1. A Deque can be used as both stack and queue

2. Maintaining History of actions

3. A steal process Scheduling Algorithms

4. Implementing a Priority Queue with two types of priorities

5. Maximum/Minimum of all sub arrays of size k in an array

Python Implementation 
# Deque : Doubly ended Queue

from collections import deque

d = deque()
d.append(10)
d.append(20)
d.append(30)

print(d)              # [10, 20, 30]

d.appendleft(100)
print(d)              # [100, 10, 20, 30]

d.pop()
print(d)              # [100, 10, 20]

d.popleft()
print(d)              # [10, 20]
Python Implementation 
d = deque([10, 20, 30, 40])
d.insert(2, 15)
print(d)              # [10, 20, 15, 30, 40]

print(d.count(10))    # 1

d.remove(10)
print(d)              # [20, 15, 30, 40]

d.extend([50, 60])
print(d)              # [20, 15, 30, 40, 50, 60]

d.extendleft([10, 20])
print(d)              # [20, 10, 20, 15, 30, 40, 50, 60]

d.reverse()
print(d)              # [60, 50, 40, 30, 15, 20, 10, 20]
Python Implementation 
from collections import deque

d = deque([10, 20, 30, 40, 50])
d.rotate(2)
print(d)              # [40, 50, 10, 20, 30]

d.rotate(-2)
print(d)              # [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]

print(d[2])           # 30
d[2] = 100
print(d)              # [10, 20, 100, 40, 50]

print(d[0])           # 10
print(d[-1])          # 50

  • count(x)
  • insert(i, x)

θ(abs(r)) :

  • rotate(x)

O(len(l)) :

  • extend(l)
  • extendleft(l)

Linked List Implementation

In O(1) Time :

  • insertFront()
  • insertRear()
  • deleteFront()
  • deleteRear()
  • getFront()
  • getRear()
  • isEmpty()
  • size()

Doubly Linked List is the best way to achieve ###

Python Implementation 
class Node:
  def __init__(self, k):
    self.key = k
    self.prev = None
    self.next = None

class Deque:
  def __init__(self):
    self.head = None
    self.rear = None
    self.size = 0

  def sizes(self):
    return self.size

  def isEmpty(self):
    return self.size == 0

  def insertRear(self, x):
    temp = Node(x)
    if self.isEmpty():
      self.head = temp
      self.rear = temp
    else:
      self.rear.next = temp
      temp.prev = self.rear
      self.rear = temp
    self.size += 1

  def insertFront(self, temp):
    temp = Node(temp)
    if self.isEmpty():
      self.head = temp
      self.rear = temp
    else:
      self.head.prev = temp
      temp.next = self.head
      self.head = temp
    self.size += 1

  def deleteFront(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return
    self.head = self.head.next
    self.head.prev = None
    self.size -= 1

  def deleteRear(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return
    self.rear = self.rear.prev
    self.rear.next = None
    self.size -= 1

  def getFront(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return
    return self.head.key

  def getRear(self):
    if self.isEmpty():
      return
    return self.rear.key

  def display(self):
    temp = self.head
    while temp:
      print(temp.key, end = " ")
      temp = temp.next
    print()

s = Deque()
s.insertRear(10)
s.insertRear(20)
s.insertRear(30)

print(s.getFront())
print(s.getRear())

s.deleteFront()

print(s.getFront())
print(s.getRear())

s.deleteRear()

print(s.getFront())
print(s.getRear())
print(s.sizes())
print(s.isEmpty())

s.insertFront(100)
s.insertFront(1000)

s.display()

List Implementation for all Operations

Python Implementation 
class myDeque:
    def __init__(self, c):
        self.items = [None] * c
        self.front = -1  # Use -1 to represent an empty deque
        self.rear = -1
        self.capacity = c

    def insertFront(self, x):
        if self.isFull():
            print("Deque is full")
            return
        elif self.isEmpty():
            self.front = self.rear = 0
        elif self.front == 0:
            self.front = self.capacity - 1
        else:
            self.front -= 1
        self.items[self.front] = x

    def insertRear(self, x):
        if self.isFull():
            print("Deque is full")
            return
        elif self.isEmpty():
            self.front = self.rear = 0
        elif self.rear == self.capacity - 1:
            self.rear = 0
        else:
            self.rear += 1
        self.items[self.rear] = x

    def deleteFront(self):
        if self.isEmpty():
            print("Deque is empty")
            return
        elif self.front == self.rear:
            self.front = self.rear = -1  # Reset deque to empty state
        elif self.front == self.capacity - 1:
            self.front = 0
        else:
            self.front += 1

    def deleteRear(self):
        if self.isEmpty():
            print("Deque is empty")
            return
        elif self.front == self.rear:
            self.front = self.rear = -1  # Reset deque to empty state
        elif self.rear == 0:
            self.rear = self.capacity - 1
        else:
            self.rear -= 1

    def getFront(self):
        if self.isEmpty():
            print("Deque is empty")
            return None
        return self.items[self.front]

    def getRear(self):
        if self.isEmpty():
            print("Deque is empty")
            return None
        return self.items[self.rear]

    def isEmpty(self):
        return self.front == -1

    def isFull(self):
        return (self.front == 0 and self.rear == self.capacity - 1) or (self.rear + 1 == self.front)

    def display(self):
        if self.isEmpty():
            print("Deque is empty")
            return
        if self.front <= self.rear:
            for i in range(self.front, self.rear + 1):
                print(self.items[i], end=" ")
        else:
            for i in range(self.front, self.capacity):
                print(self.items[i], end=" ")
            for i in range(0, self.rear + 1):
                print(self.items[i], end=" ")
        print()

# Testing the myDeque class
s = myDeque(5)
s.insertFront(10)
s.insertFront(20)
s.insertFront(30)

s.insertRear(40)
s.insertRear(50)

s.display()

s.deleteFront()
s.deleteRear()

s.display()

print(s.getFront())
print(s.getRear())
